## Where the Framing Breaks Down, An X-Plane Is Too Precious to Lose and This One Is Not

**The two halves of this article meet here.** The X series exists to fly aeroplanes that answer questions, and an aeroplane that answers questions is protected, because losing it ends the answering. **The Valkyrie exists to be lost, and its design follows from that.**

**The series supplies the contrast.** The [X-56][related_post_a353_lockheed_martin_x56] was built to flutter deliberately, as close as an X-plane comes to being expendable \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\]. One of the two airframes was destroyed on takeoff. **The programme then flew the remaining aeroplane with a caution that stopped the envelope short of the second flutter mechanism it had been built to exhibit**, because there was one aeroplane left and losing it would have ended the work \[[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned][ref_x56_lessons]\]. **A research aeroplane's value is concentrated in the airframe, so the airframe governs the risk that may be taken with it.**

**Attritability moves the value out of the airframe.** The expected-sorties relation above is the formal statement of that move. **Once the cost per sortie is low enough, the operator stops optimising for the survival of any particular aeroplane and starts optimising for the delivery of effect**, and the two optimisations give different answers about almost every decision, from how much redundancy to fit to whether to fly at all on a given night.

**The aeroplane that took the X series' number inverts the premise on which the X series decides what to do.** The distinction is sharper than manned against unmanned, or research against combat, and unlike those two it is not refuted by the register.

**It also explains the register's silence.** The mission statement for the XQ-58A says unmanned, transonic, long-range, low-cost, limited life and strike \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\]. **Two of those six words are about money and lifetime rather than about flight, and among the X rows only the X-55A names a cost, the cost of manufacturing an airframe, which it set out to compress, and none names a lifetime.** The register has columns for the date, the designation, the contractor, the engines, the sponsor and the mission. **It has no column for what the aeroplane is worth, and worth is the variable this aeroplane was designed around.**

**The claim that the Valkyrie behaves more like an X-plane than the X-planes do is defensible on the record and is not asserted here as established.** It flew in two and a half years, it was built to answer a question about cost, it carried an autonomy package nobody had flown before, and it did all of that under a designation that calls it a drone \[[Factsheet Technology, XQ-58A Valkyrie][ref_afrl_factsheet]\]. **No one at the allocating office is on record saying any of this**, and the sections above are careful to keep the two apart.

## The Source Base

**55 books and reference documents carry the argument and 2,066 research records map the field, and this article keeps them apart on purpose.** The designation half of the argument rests on four documents, being the register, the registry's missing-designations page, the registry's page for this vehicle and the governing instruction. **Those are not commentary on the evidence. They are the evidence**, because the question is what a document does and does not contain.

**The research survey holds 2,066 research records drawn from the journal and conference literature indexed by Crossref, the National Aeronautics and Space Administration's Technical Reports Server and the Defense Technical Information Center**, of which 389 are report primaries, being 18.8 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 2015.** Of the 1,995 dated records, 881 were published in 2017, the year the number was allocated, or later, being 44.6 percent.

### Where the Displayed Relations Come From

**37 of the 40 display equations carry a citation within six lines above or twelve below.** The remainder are arithmetic on a relation cited immediately above them.

**The number is reported because the alternative is to cite the same source beside every step of a derivation**, which pads a reference list without adding a source. **What would be wrong is a displayed relation whose provenance is nowhere in its section.**

### The Book Identifiers Match Their Titles and Authors

**All 12 book identifiers resolve to Open Library works whose title and author match the labels the article gives them.**

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its link is to a government report server, meaning the space agency's Technical Reports Server or a Defense Technical Information Center report, whose records carry the Digital Object Identifier prefix 10.21236. **The report-primary fraction is 18.8 percent, being 389 records of 2,066**, and by that rule 62 are held by the space agency's Technical Reports Server and 327 by the Defense Technical Information Center. **The rule does not reach the four documents that carry the designation half of the argument**, which are curated references rather than research records, so 18.8 percent understates how much of the evidence is first-hand.

**The two articles in this series whose subjects were administrative acts have report-primary fractions below three percent** \[[X-Planes: X-39, Reserved but Never Assigned][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned]\] \[[X-Planes: X-52, the Designation Refused][related_post_a349_x52_designation_refused]\]. **This article's subject is a decision about an aeroplane that exists**, and the aeroplane has a large defence-report literature, held by the defence registry far more than by the space agency.

### Thin Subjects

**Eight subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| a design number and the vehicle type symbol | designation, designations, design number, nomenclature, vehicle type symbol, mission design series | 0 |
| the attritable aircraft under its own name | attritable | 6 |
| the design life an attrition rate allows | design life, service life, fatigue life, life limit together with an unmanned-aircraft term, attritable or expendable | 0 |
| the cost per sortie | cost per sortie, cost per flight hour, cost per mission, cost per kill, cost per target, cost exchange | 1 |
| the opening load of a recovery parachute | opening load, opening shock, opening force, inflation load, opening factor, reefing, reefed | 4 |
| rocket-assisted and zero-length launch | zero length, rocket assisted, RATO, JATO, jet assisted | 6 |
| the radar cross-section of an unmanned aircraft | radar cross section, RCS, stealth, low observable together with an unmanned-aircraft term | 15 |
| the learning curve of an airframe | learning curve, experience curve, progress ratio, cost improvement curve together with aircraft, airframe, aerospace, airplane, weapon, defense, military | 2 |

**No research title in the survey contains a word beginning with designat, or the words design number, nomenclature, vehicle type symbol or mission design series.** **The designation half of this article rests on four primary documents and not on the survey**, and it would say exactly the same things if the survey were deleted.

**Of the 2,066 records in the reference list, the number whose title contains the word attritable is 6.** That is the word the laboratory built the programme around and put in its portfolio name. **Widening to expendable, disposable and attrition-tolerant reaches 15, and the phrase low cost, counted on its own, appears in 75.** **A concept with a programme, an aeroplane and a fact sheet has almost no literature under its own name and a large one under a plainer word**, and the name that hides it is the Air Force's own.

**The only research record that studies this aeroplane had to build something that resembles it first.** It computes a radar cross-section for a computer-aided design model its own title calls Valkyrie-like \[[Max Chung and Tuan 2020][research_maxchung_tuan_2020]\], and it is the one record of the 2,066 whose title names the Valkyrie. **15 titles join radar cross-section, stealth or low observability to an unmanned aircraft, and none of them names this aeroplane**, which is why the relation between survivability and its price is displayed rather than evaluated.

**No title joins a design life, a service life or a fatigue life to an unmanned, attritable or expendable aircraft**, so the argument that the design life should be matched to the survival probability stands on the expected-sorties relation derived above rather than on a published analysis. **One title names a cost per sortie or a cost exchange, and it proposes an aggregate measure of cost and operational effectiveness rather than analysing an aeroplane.**

**Of the 142 titles that name a parachute, a canopy, a drogue or a parafoil, 4 name an opening load, an opening factor or reefing, and none of those 4 concerns an unmanned aircraft.** The finding that the recovery must be staged therefore rests on the opening-load relation and not on a literature of unmanned-aircraft recovery. **Six titles name rocket-assisted, jet-assisted or zero-length launch, and four of them concern an unmanned aircraft.**

**40 titles name a learning or experience curve, and 2 of them join it to an aircraft, an airframe, an aerospace product, a weapon or the defence industry**, so the statement that airframe learning curves run at 80 to 85 percent rests on the standard texts rather than on this survey.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** The survey's 2,066 records come from the National Aeronautics and Space Administration's Technical Reports Server, the Defense Technical Information Center and the journal and conference literature indexed by Crossref. Correction, erratum and withdrawal notices, conference front matter, reviews and trade items are excluded, because a survey counts research works and those are editorial events rather than works, and a document deposited twice, as a preprint and as its published version or as two entries of one encyclopaedia, is counted once. Dictionary and other reference entries of two pages or fewer are excluded on the same ground, while an authored encyclopedia article of more than two pages and a textbook chapter are research works and stay. So are the listing of a document in an abstract journal, the bare text of a resolution and a book's directory of organisations.

**Many words this subject depends on also name something else, and a record that shares only such a word is excluded.** Valkyrie is also an opera by Wagner, the 1944 Stauffenberg plot, an America's Cup yacht, a fuzzing tool, a quadruped robot, a novel and **the space agency's own humanoid robot**. Kratos is also the Greek personification of power, the word being ordinary Greek for strength, and the string Xq is also the long arm of the human X chromosome. **Expendable in aerospace usually means a rocket**, and the word also names expendable bathythermographs, batteries and infrared sensors, so a launch context excludes it while an expendable turbojet for an unmanned aircraft still passes. Attrition also means personnel turnover, the loss of a workforce or a language, and the loss of trainees from a training programme, although a study of personnel attrition rates in historical land combat is itself the subject. Survivability also names the survivability of an information system, a communications network, a munition, a ground vehicle, a ship and a strategic structure. Cost-effectiveness also names studies of medical treatment, of military training and of other industries, among them furniture, food processors and drainage structures. Lanchester also names an actress, an architect and a novelist, and the same Lanchester's theory of the phugoid is a different subject, Breguet also names a watchmaker and an aircraft manufacturer, relay also names molecular signalling and frame relay, task allocation also names computing jobs and software teams, an exchange ratio also belongs to respiratory physiology and Monte Carlo sampling, and teaming also names human-robot teams in factory assembly. Autonomous road vehicles, low-cost airlines, the low-cost manufacture of printed electronic sensors, diesel and garden engines, and standard atmospheres for conditioning paper samples are excluded for the same reason.

**Records whose only link to the article is the name of a method, or a distant application of an unmanned aircraft, are excluded as well.** Task allocation, trajectory planning and cooperative relaying that name no aircraft, unmanned vehicle or military setting are excluded, because the article names these methods and does not apply them, and so are combat models of computer games. Civil applications of unmanned aircraft whose subject is the data they collect, such as crop, forest and flood survey, are excluded, and so are civil regulation, airspace integration and certification, because the argument uses none of them, and ground, surface and underwater robots on their own.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept.** The learning curve is a method this article applies, so its literature in other fields is kept, among it a paper on the learning curve of the microwave oven. **A method the article uses brings its literature with it.** The Lanchester attrition model is kept in every combat setting, because the exchange relation the article states for the cost-imposing claim belongs to the same family. The parachutes and airbags of spacecraft and boosters are kept because the opening-load and touchdown relations the article derives are the same, and so are the industry practices for the engines, launchers and parachutes of unmanned aircraft. Human-autonomy teaming studies that name no setting are kept as neighbouring work. Curriculum learning for air-combat autonomy and a comparison of the Breguet equation with numerical evaluation of a cruise segment are on the subject and are kept, although their titles share words with excluded classes.

**The residual cluster admits a title on the engineering of an unmanned aircraft that names no subject a cluster above claims**, and it holds 140 records, being 6.8 percent of the survey.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

## Epistemic State

### Historical Fact

**The XQ-58A designation was allocated on 10-Jul-17 to Composite Engineering with the United States Air Force as sponsor** \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\]. **The X-57A was allocated on 03-May-16 and the X-59A on 21-Jun-18** \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\]. **There is no X-58 row in the register**, and the string appears once in the file, in a note saying it does not exist.

**The aeroplane first flew on 5 March 2019 at Yuma Proving Ground** \[[XQ-58A Valkyrie demonstrator completes inaugural flight][ref_first_flight]\]. **The contract preceding it was awarded in 2016 under the Low Cost Attritable Strike Demonstrator programme, within the Low Cost Attritable Aircraft Technology portfolio** \[[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_ds_q58]\] \[[Factsheet Technology, XQ-58A Valkyrie][ref_afrl_factsheet]\].

**The governing instruction requires a basic or modified mission symbol to accompany a vehicle type symbol, at tier T-0, and separately reserves to the allocating office the authority to skip design numbers at discretion** \[[DAFI 16-401][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]\].

### Verified by Reading the Document

**The register, in the copy last updated on 23 August 2026, carries 539 allocation rows and 31 of them are X designations.** **15 of those 31 describe an unmanned aeroplane and zero of them carry the Q vehicle type symbol.** Both counts come from the register, and neither is taken from a secondary source. Under the version current on this article's date the register carried 526 rows and twenty-nine X rows, 13 of them unmanned, as the opening sets out \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, 11 August 2025][ref_mds_addendum_2025]\].

**The three gaps in the X run from 44 to 68 are 52, 58 and 67.** **The out-of-sequence Q numbers inside the range the X series occupies are 42, 44, 48, 58, 67, 72 and 73, of which 58, 67, 72 and 73 are X gaps.**

### Analysis

**The rate at which the X series consumes numbers is 1.385 per year over 26 years, so one skipped number costs about 8.7 months of allocation and the sequence has about 666.6 years of headroom.** The figures follow from the register's dates, and they assume that the past rate continues without predicting that it will.

**The lift-to-drag ratio the published range requires is 10.43 at a consumption of 0.80 per hour and 13.69 at 1.05 per hour.** The ceiling requires between 7.65 and 12.53 depending on the thrust lapse law assumed. **The two agree to within the width of the assumptions, so the check establishes consistency and leaves the aeroplane unmeasured.**

**The booster propellant needed to reach a lift-off speed between 40 and 70 m per second is between 1.77 and 3.1 percent of the maximum mass.** This ignores engine thrust, drag and rail friction along the launch run, all of which reduce the requirement, so it is an upper bound.

**Several further results are arithmetic on published figures.** **The published maximum speed of 566 knots is Mach 0.856 at sea level and Mach 0.987 at the quoted ceiling**, so it agrees with the published Mach 0.85 at the first and not the second. **The two published unit costs, read as a learning curve, imply a progress ratio of 0.5** against the 80 to 85 percent the standard texts quote, and an 85 percent curve would need 4.27 doublings rather than one.

**Once the design life stops binding, the cost per sortie is the unit cost multiplied by the probability of loss, so the derivative with respect to survivability is the unit cost itself.** A percentage point is worth 20,000 dollars a sortie at the lower unit cost. **The break-even against an aeroplane that is not meant to be lost depends only on the ratio of the two unit costs and not on either price.**

**The canopy that gives an eight metre per second touchdown, opened at 60 metres per second, would impose 56.3 times gravity.** **No airframe survives that**, so the recovery must be staged, and a first stage of about 0.1778 of the full area holds the load to ten times gravity.

**The cruise model closes against its own input to within two percent**, returning 5,451 kilometres against a published 5,556.

**One result rests on a primary statement of cost, the sharpest in the article.** A congressional research product records that the Air Force has stated a collaborative combat aircraft would cost roughly one third the price of a crewed fighter \[[U.S. Air Force Collaborative Combat Aircraft, CRS IF12740, 15 August 2024][ref_crs_cca_2024]\]. **At that cost ratio of three, the break-even relation demands a survival probability of 0.97 against a fighter surviving 0.99.** **Against an assumed eighty million dollar fighter, the demonstrator's published unit costs give a ratio of 20 to 40**, and that assumption is flagged wherever it is used.

### Inference

**That the number 58 was taken from the X series is an inference from three dates, made by the registry before this article made it.** No allocation letter is public. **The inference is strong but falls short of proof.**

**That the Valkyrie's Q designation follows from its carrying the vehicle type symbol that the fifteen unmanned X rows omit is this article's inference, and a weaker one.** It is consistent with the register and stated nowhere in the record. **Nobody has been found saying it.**

**That a programme expecting to be procured had a reason to prefer a designation that survives the transition to service is a hypothesis and is labelled as one.** The MQ-58B exists and the X-35 became the F-35, so the mechanism is real \[[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_ds_q58]\] \[[Current Designations of U.S. Military Aircraft][ref_mds_aircraft]\]. **Whether it operated in 2017 is unknown.**

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**Why the letters are XQ rather than X is unclear, and unclear is the registry's word** \[[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_ds_q58]\].

**Whether X in XQ-58A is the status prefix or the basic mission symbol is not stated anywhere consulted.** The sibling designations favour the status prefix and the shape of the instruction's example favours the basic mission. **Under the first reading the designation omits a symbol the instruction requires at its highest tier. Under the second it is well formed and its number is still wrong.**

**Whether the engine is a turbojet or a turbofan is not settled by any primary document found here.** The arithmetic favours the turbofan and does not decide it.

**No source states that the maximum speed is a sea level figure.** The article infers it because the alternative puts a subsonic airframe at Mach 0.987. **The inference comes from consistency, and nobody has stated it as fact.**

**Whether the two published unit costs are cumulative-unit figures or annual-rate figures is not stated**, and the two readings describe different mechanisms. **The article reports both and settles neither.**

**On what basis the payload fraction is quoted is not stated either**, so the comparison with the figure the [X-46][related_post_a343_boeing_x46] measured may be comparing two different things.

**No consulted source documents how the parachute recovery is staged.** The article derives that it must be staged and does not report how.

**Which loading the published range assumes is not stated**, and the answer changes the implied lift-to-drag ratio by nearly a factor of two.

**What a collaborative combat aircraft will cost is not settled by anything consulted.** The service's stated ratio of one third is a statement of intent recorded by a research service, not a price.

**What the aeroplane costs to operate is not published**, so every cost-per-sortie figure in this article is airframe amortisation alone. **The fuel component can be bounded, being about half a percent of the amortisation at an assumed dollar a kilogram and under six percent at ten times that price**, which narrows the gap without closing it. **Maintenance, ground equipment, boosters, parachutes and people remain unquantified**, and the low maintenance the fact sheet claims has no public figure behind it.

**The radar cross-section is not published**, so the relation between survivability and its price is displayed as a mechanism and never evaluated for this aeroplane.

## Out of Scope

**The tactics of manned and unmanned teaming are not treated here.** The literature is large and the survey in The Contemporary Literature maps it, but the question this article asks is about a designation and an aeroplane, not about how to fight with one.

**The Collaborative Combat Aircraft programme is named and not examined.** It postdates the allocation by years and its competition, its increments and its contractors belong to a different article. **One number about it is used**, being the cost ratio the service has stated to Congress, and its only use is to put a value into a relation this article derives about the demonstrator. **Whether the programme's economics work is a question this article asks and does not answer.**

**The autonomy software is named and not examined.** What an autonomy package does, how it is tested and whether it can be trusted are questions this article touches only where they bear on why the airframe exists.

**The X-59 is named only as the next allocation in the register.** It is the subject of the next article in this series and nothing is said about it here beyond its date and its designation.

**The classified portion of the designation record is not addressed**, because there is no way to address it. The register is the public record and this article treats it as complete for the numbers it discusses while noting that it is a supplement rather than a full list.

## Conclusion

**The X-58 does not exist, the number 58 does, and the record shows the second without explaining the first.** The registry states that the slot was skipped and that the number was taken from the X series by the Kratos Valkyrie \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\] \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\]. **The governing instruction shows how such an absence is permitted, reserving to the allocating office the authority to skip a design number at discretion and requiring no reason to be recorded** \[[DAFI 16-401][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]\].

**What the record does not contain is the exercise of that authority.** No allocation letter for the X-58 is public, and none is likely to exist, because a number consumed by another series does not need to be refused. **The article therefore holds that the absence is permitted and unexplained, rather than that somebody decided it.**

**It went to an aeroplane that flew.** The XQ-58A was allocated fourteen months after the [X-57][related_post_a354_esaero_x57_maxwell] and flew four years before that programme closed without flying at all \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\] \[[X-57 Project Creates Paths Toward Electric Aviation][ref_nasa_x57_paths]\]. **The number the X series gave up was spent better than the number it kept.**

**What the series lost by giving it up was about 8.7 months of numbering out of six and a half centuries of supply**, so scarcity explains nothing and no argument in this article rests on it.

**Every obvious reason for the Valkyrie not being an X-plane fails against the register.** Nearly half the X rows describe an unmanned aeroplane. One X row is an armed unmanned combat air vehicle. Another was allocated for the same mission the production Valkyrie is intended to fly \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\] \[[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_ds_q58]\]. **The register refutes the manned test, the combat test and the armament test in turn, and it does so with its own rows.**

**What survives is a distinction the register cannot record.** An X-plane's value sits in its airframe, so the airframe governs the risk. **An attritable aeroplane's value sits in the effect it delivers, and the airframe is a consumable.** The [X-56][related_post_a353_lockheed_martin_x56] stopped short of the boundary it was built to cross because one airframe was left \[[Miller, X-56 Flight Test and Lessons Learned][ref_x56_lessons]\]. **The Valkyrie is designed so that the same question never arises.**

**The arithmetic added one thing the prose could not have reached, the argument for attritable aircraft stated without a price.** Once the design life stops binding, the cost per sortie is the unit cost multiplied by the probability of loss. **The break-even against an aeroplane that is not meant to be lost therefore depends only on the ratio of the two costs**, and needs the price of neither.

**An honest use of that relation puts the stated number into it and leaves the flattering ones out.** The Air Force has told Congress that a collaborative combat aircraft would cost roughly one third the price of a crewed fighter \[[U.S. Air Force Collaborative Combat Aircraft, CRS IF12740, 15 August 2024][ref_crs_cca_2024]\]. **At a cost ratio of three, such an aeroplane must survive 0.97 of its sorties to beat a fighter that survives 0.99 of its own.** **That is a fighter's number, not an attritable one.**

**The demonstrator's published unit costs give a ratio of 20 to 40 against an assumed eighty million dollar fighter**, and at twenty the requirement falls to 0.8. **The relation is the same and the answer is not, because attritability belongs to a price and not to a shape.**

**So the relation shows when the case fails as clearly as when it holds**, and a programme brochure leaves that half out. **The argument needs a hostile sky and a large cost ratio together**, and an aeroplane at a third the price of a fighter, flown where fighters mostly come home, has neither.

**The register does record one thing, the shape of a system losing a rule.** Four of the seven out-of-sequence Q numbers inside the X range are X gaps, one follows a fighter, the registry gives no account of the other two, and the registry has already written of the fighters that there is to all intents and purposes no longer a sequence \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\]. **A design number was once a position in a queue. It is becoming a thing a programme chooses because of what it signals.**

**The X-58 is not the first place in the X series where a number stopped being the next one and became the right one. The register shows two earlier cases.**

**The registry names the X-50A as a special number**, allocated on 13-Feb-02, 15 years earlier, and this series has already written that article \[[Current Designations of U.S. Military Aircraft][ref_mds_aircraft]\] \[[X-Planes: Boeing X-50 Dragonfly][related_post_a347_boeing_x50]\]. **The XQ-58A is not the first of its family either**, the XRQ-72A having been allocated on 20-Mar-17, 4 months before it, on a number that is also a gap in the X series \[[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\].

**The true claim is narrower, and the article can support it.** The XQ-58A is the earliest allocation whose number the registry states outright was taken from the X series, the 72 being recorded as unclear and the 73 as probable \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\]. **It is the first case where the registry is certain, not the first case.**

**The X-58 is not a missing aeroplane.** It is a number that was spent in another series while it was still the next one due, by an authority that was not obliged to say so and did not.

## Equations

<!-- Equations generated by _lib/equations.py from _sources/equations/. Edit the data file and regenerate. Do not edit this section by hand. -->

This section collects the 40 display equations of the article in the order they appear,
each under its number and reproduced exactly as it stands in the text.
A title in bold names an established result,
and a title in italics beginning with the word Bespoke names a construction made for this analysis.
Of the 40, 28 are established and 12 are bespoke.
Masses are in kilograms and forces in newtons, speeds in metres per second although the specification quotes knots, costs in dollars, design-number rates in numbers per year and probabilities per sortie, and the attrition and cost relations rest on a constant and independent probability of surviving each sortie, which is a modelling choice rather than a measured property of the aeroplane.

**Equation 1, Rate of Design Number Consumption.**
The mean rate at which the X series consumes numbers is the span of design numbers in the register divided by the span of dates, which evaluates to 1.385 numbers per year.
[Parsch, DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]

$$
\dot{d} \;=\; \frac{d_L - d_F}{t_L - t_F}
\tag{1}
$$

*Equation 2, Bespoke Headroom and Cost of One Skipped Number.*
Extrapolating the mean rate as constant, an assumption the article makes, gives the time remaining before the field reaches 999 and the allocation time that one skipped number costs.
[DAFI 16-401][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]

$$
t_{999} \;=\; \frac{999 - d_L}{\dot{d}} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \Delta t_s \;=\; \frac{1}{\dot{d}}
\tag{2}
$$

**Equation 3, Mass Budget.**
The maximum mass is the sum of the empty mass, the payload and the fuel.

$$
W_0 \;=\; W_e \;+\; W_p \;+\; W_f
\tag{3}
$$

**Equation 4, Fuel Fraction.**
The fuel fraction is one less the empty and payload fractions of the maximum mass, 38.3 percent with both payload stations filled.
[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_ds_q58]
[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_xq58_wikipedia]

$$
\zeta \;=\; \frac{W_f}{W_0} \;=\; 1 \;-\; \frac{W_e + W_p}{W_0}
\tag{4}
$$

**Equation 5, Payload Fraction.**
The payload fraction is the payload weight over the maximum weight, 20 percent for the Valkyrie.
[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_ds_q58]
[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_xq58_wikipedia]

$$
\Pi_p \;=\; \frac{W_p}{W_0}
\tag{5}
$$

**Equation 6, Speed of Sound.**
The speed of sound in air depends only on temperature through the ratio of specific heats and the specific gas constant.

$$
a_s \;=\; \sqrt{\gamma \, \mathcal{R} \, \Theta}
\tag{6}
$$

**Equation 7, Mach Number.**
The Mach number is the true airspeed over the local speed of sound.
[U.S. Standard Atmosphere 1976 1976][ref_us_standard_atmosphere]
[International Standard Atmosphere][ref_icao_atmosphere]

$$
M \;=\; \frac{V}{a_s} \;=\; \frac{V}{\sqrt{\gamma \, \mathcal{R} \, \Theta}}
\tag{7}
$$

**Equation 8, Dynamic Pressure in Terms of Static Pressure and Mach Number.**
The dynamic pressure is half the density times the square of the airspeed, or half the ratio of specific heats times the static pressure times the square of the Mach number.

$$
q \;=\; \tfrac{1}{2} \, \rho \, V^{2} \;=\; \tfrac{1}{2} \, \gamma \, p \, M^{2}
\tag{8}
$$

*Equation 9, Bespoke Expected Sorties per Airframe.*
Taking a constant and independent probability of surviving each sortie, the article writes the expected sorties of an airframe with a finite design life as a truncated geometric sum.
[Geometric distribution][ref_geometric_distribution]

$$
\bar{s} \;=\; \sum_{k=1}^{N} p^{\,k-1} \;=\; \frac{1 - p^{N}}{1 - p}
\tag{9}
$$

*Equation 10, Bespoke Attrition-Limited Life.*
As the design life grows without bound the expected sorties under the constant survival model tend to one over the probability of loss.

$$
\lim_{N \to \infty} \bar{s} \;=\; \frac{1}{1-p}
\tag{10}
$$

*Equation 11, Bespoke Matched Design Life and Life Utilisation.*
The article sets the design life equal to the attrition-limited life and defines the fraction of the design life actually used under the same model.

$$
N^{\star} \;\simeq\; \frac{1}{1-p} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \eta_L \;=\; \frac{\bar{s}}{N} \;=\; \frac{1-p^{N}}{N\,(1-p)}
\tag{11}
$$

*Equation 12, Bespoke Probability of Reaching the Design Life.*
Under independent sorties an airframe reaches its design life with probability equal to the survival probability raised to that life.

$$
\Pr\{\text{the airframe reaches } N\} \;=\; p^{N}
\tag{12}
$$

*Equation 13, Bespoke Expected Cost per Sortie.*
The article amortises the unit cost over the expected sorties of the survival model and adds the operating cost of one sortie.

$$
\bar{C} \;=\; \frac{C_a}{\bar{s}} \;+\; C_{op} \;=\; \frac{C_a\,(1-p)}{1 - p^{N}} \;+\; C_{op}
\tag{13}
$$

*Equation 14, Bespoke Cost per Sortie When the Life Does Not Bind.*
Once the design life stops binding, the amortised cost tends to the unit cost times the probability of loss, so a point of survival probability is worth one percent of the airframe price per sortie.
[Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie][ref_xq58_wikipedia]

$$
\bar{C} \;\to\; C_a \, (1-p) \qquad\text{and}\qquad \frac{\partial \bar{C}}{\partial p} \;=\; -\, C_a
\tag{14}
$$

*Equation 15, Bespoke Break-Even Survival Probability Against a Costlier Aircraft.*
Equating the limiting costs per sortie of the attritable aircraft and an aircraft not meant to be lost gives the survival probability at which the two cost the same, which depends only on the ratio of unit costs.

$$
C_a \, (1 - p) \;<\; C_x \, (1 - p_x) \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad p^{\star} \;=\; 1 \;-\; \frac{C_x}{C_a} \, (1 - p_x)
\tag{15}
$$

*Equation 16, Bespoke Airframes Needed for a Campaign.*
Dividing the sorties a campaign requires by the expected sorties per airframe of the survival model, rounded up, gives the airframes to buy.

$$
n_{ac} \;=\; \left\lceil \frac{S_{req}}{\bar{s}} \right\rceil
\tag{16}
$$

**Equation 17, Learning Curve.**
Unit cost falls with cumulative production as a power of the unit number, with an exponent set by the progress ratio.
[Experience curve effects][ref_learning_curve]
[Roskam, Airplane design part VIII, airplane cost estimation][book_roskam]

$$
C_u \;=\; C_1 \, u^{\,\beta}, \qquad \beta \;=\; \frac{\ln \phi}{\ln 2}
\tag{17}
$$

**Equation 18, Progress Ratio From Two Unit Costs.**
Inverting the learning curve gives the progress ratio from two unit costs and the number of doublings between their production quantities.

$$
\phi \;=\; \left( \frac{C_B}{C_A} \right)^{\!\!\frac{1}{\log_2 (u_B / u_A)}}
\tag{18}
$$

**Equation 19, Doublings Needed to Halve the Unit Cost.**
The number of doublings of output needed to halve the unit cost is the logarithm of one half over the logarithm of the progress ratio.
[Roskam, Airplane design part VIII, airplane cost estimation][book_roskam]
[Nicolai and Carichner, Fundamentals of aircraft and airship design][book_nicolai]

$$
\nu \;=\; \frac{\ln \tfrac{1}{2}}{\ln \phi}
\tag{19}
$$

*Equation 20, Bespoke Cost-Imposing Exchange Condition.*
The article states the exchange as favouring the attacker when the cost of the interceptors expended exceeds the airframe cost amortised over its expected sorties.
[Factsheet Technology, XQ-58A Valkyrie][ref_afrl_factsheet]

$$
n_i \, C_i \;>\; \frac{C_a}{\bar{s}}
\tag{20}
$$

*Equation 21, Bespoke Comparison With a Cruise Missile.*
The attritable aircraft is preferred when its amortised airframe cost per weapon delivered plus the weapon cost is below the cost of a cruise missile, a condition the article constructs.

$$
\frac{C_a}{\bar{s}\,N_w} \;+\; C_w \;<\; C_m
\tag{21}
$$

**Equation 22, Fourth-Root Dependence of Detection Range on Cross-Section.**
Because returned power falls as the fourth power of range, detection range varies as the fourth root of the radar cross-section.
[Knott, Shaeffer and Tuley, Radar cross section][book_knott]

$$
R_d \;\propto\; \Sigma^{\,1/4} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{\Sigma'}{\Sigma} \;=\; \left( \frac{R_d'}{R_d} \right)^{\!4}
\tag{22}
$$

**Equation 23, Impulse Balance Along the Launch Rail.**
The booster impulse equals the momentum at lift-off less the net impulse of engine thrust, drag and rail friction during the run.

$$
\int_0^{t_b} T_b \, \mathrm{d}t \;=\; m \, V_{LO} \;-\; \int_0^{t_b} \left( T_e - D - \mu \, F_N \right) \mathrm{d}t
\tag{23}
$$

*Equation 24, Bespoke Upper Bound on the Booster Impulse.*
Discarding the engine contribution and the losses together, a simplification the article adopts, bounds the required impulse by the launch mass times the lift-off speed.

$$
I_{req} \;\le\; m_0 \, V_{LO}
\tag{24}
$$

**Equation 25, Propellant Mass From Specific Impulse.**
The propellant needed is the required impulse divided by standard gravity times the specific impulse.
[Specific impulse][ref_specific_impulse]

$$
m_p \;=\; \frac{I_{req}}{g_0 \, I_{sp}}
\tag{25}
$$

**Equation 26, Launch Rail Length.**
Under constant acceleration the rail length is the square of the lift-off speed over twice the acceleration.

$$
L_r \;=\; \frac{V_{LO}^{2}}{2 \, a}
\tag{26}
$$

**Equation 27, Terminal Velocity Under a Canopy.**
The steady descent speed under a canopy follows from equating weight to the canopy drag.
[Knacke, Parachute recovery systems design manual][book_knacke]

$$
V_t \;=\; \sqrt{\frac{2 \, W}{\rho \, C_{D_p} \, S_p}}
\tag{27}
$$

**Equation 28, Canopy Area and Equivalent Diameter.**
Solving the descent relation for the canopy gives the area that a chosen touchdown speed requires and its equivalent circular diameter.
[Knacke, Parachute recovery systems design manual][book_knacke]

$$
S_p \;=\; \frac{2 \, W}{\rho \, C_{D_p} \, V_t^{2}} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad D_p \;=\; \sqrt{\frac{4 \, S_p}{\pi}}
\tag{28}
$$

**Equation 29, Parachute Opening Force.**
The opening force is the steady canopy drag at the deployment speed multiplied by a measured opening shock factor.
[Knacke, Parachute recovery systems design manual][book_knacke]
[Wolf and Fallon 2011][research_wolf_fallon_2011]

$$
F \;=\; k_s \, \tfrac{1}{2} \, \rho \, V_d^{2} \, C_{D_p} \, S_p
\tag{29}
$$

**Equation 30, Deployment Speed Limit for a Permitted Load Factor.**
Solving the opening force for speed gives the highest speed at which the full canopy may be opened without exceeding a load factor on the empty mass.

$$
V_d \;\le\; \sqrt{\frac{2 \, n_g \, m_e \, g_0}{\rho \, C_{D_p} \, S_p}}
\tag{30}
$$

**Equation 31, Reefed Canopy Area for a Permitted Load Factor.**
The reefed area that holds the opening load to a permitted load factor at a given deployment speed follows from the same relation.
[Knacke, Parachute recovery systems design manual][book_knacke]

$$
\frac{S_r}{S_p} \;=\; \frac{2 \, n_g \, m_e \, g_0}{\rho \, C_{D_p} \, V_d^{2} \, S_p}
\tag{31}
$$

**Equation 32, Drift Under the Canopy.**
The downwind drift is the wind speed times the descent time, the release height over the descent speed.

$$
x_d \;=\; V_w \, \frac{h_d}{V_t}
\tag{32}
$$

**Equation 33, Ratio of Cruise to Touchdown Kinetic Energy.**
The ratio of kinetic energies at cruise and at touchdown is the mass ratio times the square of the speed ratio.

$$
\frac{E_c}{E_t} \;=\; \frac{m_0}{m_e} \left( \frac{V}{V_t} \right)^{2}
\tag{33}
$$

**Equation 34, Breguet Range Equation for a Jet.**
The jet range is the speed over the thrust specific fuel consumption times the lift-to-drag ratio times the logarithm of the weight ratio.
[Anderson, Aircraft performance and design][book_anderson]
[Mattingly, Heiser and Pratt, Aircraft engine design][book_mattingly]

$$
R \;=\; \frac{V}{c}\,\frac{L}{D}\,\ln\!\left(\frac{W_0}{W_1}\right)
\tag{34}
$$

**Equation 35, Lift-to-Drag Ratio Required by the Published Range.**
Rearranging the range equation gives the lift-to-drag ratio that the published range, cruise speed and fuel fraction demand at a given consumption.

$$
\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_{\text{req}} \;=\; \frac{R\,c}{V\,\ln\!\left(\dfrac{W_0}{W_0 - W_f}\right)}
\tag{35}
$$

**Equation 36, Thrust Required in Cruise.**
In steady level cruise the thrust required is the weight over the lift-to-drag ratio.

$$
T_{req} \;=\; \frac{W}{L/D}
\tag{36}
$$

**Equation 37, Cruise Fuel Flow and Endurance.**
The fuel flow is the consumption times the thrust required, and the endurance is the fuel weight over that flow.

$$
\dot{W}_f \;=\; c \, T_{req} \qquad\text{and}\qquad t \;=\; \frac{W_f}{c \, T_{req}}
\tag{37}
$$

**Equation 38, Thrust Lapse With Density.**
Available thrust falls with altitude as a power of the density ratio set by a lapse exponent.
[Mattingly, Heiser and Pratt, Aircraft engine design][book_mattingly]

$$
T(h) \;=\; T_0 \, \sigma^{\,\kappa}, \qquad \sigma \;=\; \frac{\rho(h)}{\rho_0}
\tag{38}
$$

**Equation 39, Lift-to-Drag Ratio Implied by the Ceiling.**
At the ceiling the lapsed thrust equals the drag, which fixes the lift-to-drag ratio needed to hold it.
[Mattingly, Heiser and Pratt, Aircraft engine design][book_mattingly]

$$
T_0 \, \sigma^{\,\kappa} \;=\; D \;=\; \frac{W}{L/D} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_{\text{ceil}} \;=\; \frac{W}{T_0 \, \sigma^{\,\kappa}}
\tag{39}
$$

**Equation 40, Density Ratio of the Standard Atmosphere.**
The standard atmosphere gives the density ratio as a power law of temperature in the troposphere and an exponential decay in the isothermal layer above it.
[U.S. Standard Atmosphere 1976 1976][ref_us_standard_atmosphere]
[International Standard Atmosphere][ref_icao_atmosphere]

$$
\sigma \;=\; \left(1 - \frac{\lambda h}{\Theta_0}\right)^{\frac{g}{\lambda \mathcal{R}} - 1} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \sigma \;=\; \sigma_{11} \, \exp\!\left(-\frac{g\,(h - h_{11})}{\mathcal{R}\,\Theta_{11}}\right)
\tag{40}
$$

### Notation

- $\dot{d}$, the rate at which the series consumes design numbers
- $d_L$, the highest design number allocated, with $d_F$ the design number of the earliest X allocation
- $t_{999}$, the time for the sequence to reach the end of its field
- $\Delta t_s$, the allocation time one skipped number costs
- $W_0$, the weight at the start of cruise, with $W_1$ the weight at the end
- $W_e$, the empty weight
- $W_p$, the payload weight
- $W_f$, the weight of the fuel
- $\zeta$, the fuel fraction
- $\Pi_p$, the payload fraction
- $a_s$, the speed of sound
- $\Theta$, the air temperature
- $\mathcal{R}$, the specific gas constant for air
- $\bar{s}$, the expected number of sorties one airframe delivers
- $p$, the probability of surviving one sortie
- $N^{\star}$, the design life matched to the survival probability, with $N$ the design life in sorties
- $\eta_L$, the fraction of the design life actually used
- $\bar{C}$, the expected cost per sortie
- $C_a$, the unit cost of one airframe
- $C_{op}$, the operating cost of one sortie
- $p^{\star}$, the survival probability at which the two aeroplanes cost the same
- $C_x$, the unit cost of an aeroplane that is not meant to be lost, with $p_x$ its probability of surviving one sortie
- $n_{ac}$, the airframes needed
- $S_{req}$, the sorties the campaign requires
- $\phi$, the progress ratio, the cost factor per doubling of output
- $\nu$, the doublings of output needed to halve the unit cost
- $n_i$, the interceptors expended to remove one aeroplane, each of cost $C_i$
- $N_w$, the weapons delivered per sortie
- $R_d$, the range at which the vehicle is detected
- $\Sigma$, the radar cross-section
- $V_{LO}$, the lift-off speed
- $I_{req}$, the booster impulse required
- $I_{sp}$, the booster specific impulse
- $L_r$, the launch rail length
- $V_t$, the steady descent speed under the canopy
- $C_{D_p}$, the canopy drag coefficient
- $S_p$, the canopy area
- $k_s$, the opening shock factor
- $V_d$, the speed at which the canopy is deployed
- $n_g$, the load factor the airframe may take on opening
- $S_r$, the reefed canopy area
- $x_d$, the distance the vehicle drifts under the canopy
- $E_c$, the kinetic energy in cruise, with $E_t$ that at touchdown
- $c$, the thrust specific fuel consumption
- $T_{req}$, the thrust required in cruise
- $\kappa$, the thrust lapse exponent
- $\sigma$, the density ratio
